SC held this was NOT appropriate as general answer to chronic pain, which often persisted beyond 4-week period. Restriction on benefits for chronic pain distinguished btw workers with chronic pain and workers with other kinds of workrelated injuries. Distinction based on disability, even though members of comparison group also disabled. Distinction also impaired the human dignity of chronic pain sufferers & thus counted as discrimination that was prohibited by Ss. 15. It could NOT be justified under Ss. 1

226

   

SC struck down the provision and postponed the declaration of invalidity for 6 months. Criticism – should have deferred to legislative judgment. SC acknowledged that chronic pain has no physical manifestations, that there is no received method of diagnosis or treatment, that false claims are hard to detect and that “the medical evidence before us does point to early intervention and return to work as the most promising treatment for chronic pain” SC also acknowledged that the benefits of gov’t programs cannot be “fully customized”. However, SC ordering customized scheme & was dismissive of concerns based on cost & admin expediency.

Eldridge v BC [1997]   SC held that the administrators of BC’s health services plan had NOT accommodated the special needs of deaf people seeking medical services, because they were NOT providing sign-language interpretation. SC held this was a breach of Ss. 15; because communication was a crucial part of most medical services, it was a denial of equal benefit to deaf people not to provide the assistance that would enable effective communication to occur btw a deaf patient and a hospital or doctor.

Rodriguez v BC [1993]    P, who suffered from a debilitating, fatal disease (Lou Gehrig’s disease), challenged constitutionality of CCd offence of assisting a person to commit suicide. This provision had the effect of prohibiting the commission of suicide by a person so disabled that they couldn’t do it themselves, when non-disabled person were free to commit suicide by themselves. SC upheld the provision. Sopinka J, for the majority, did NOT deal with this argument, contenting himself that the prohibition would in any case be justified under Ss. 1

55.20 Citizenship
***Andrews v Law Society of BC [1989]  BC law requiring a person be a Canadian citizen as a qualification for admission to the bar of the province was a breach of Ss. 15 that was NOT justified by Ss. 1

Andrews followed in Lavoie v Canada [2002]  Was discrimination under Ss. 15 BUT upholding statutory hiring preference in the federal public service under Ss. 1

55.21 Marital status
Miron v Trudel [1995]   SC held marital status was an analogous ground and that Ontario’s Insurance Act offended Ss. 15 by limiting accident benefits to the legally-married spouse of an insured, which had the effect of excluding CL spouses. After this case, SC added human dignity element. This added some protection to the institution of marriage.

Nova Scotia v Walsh [2002]     Challenge to NS’s matrimonial property law, which came into effect on breakdown of marriage, NOT CL rel’p SC decided that the exclusion of CL rel’p did NOT impair human dignity, because the law was premised on the assumption that ONLY those persons who had made the choice to get married had committed themselves to a rel’p of such permanence that would justify imposing on them obligations to contribute to & share each other’s assets. Mere choice to live together, w/o getting married, could properly be viewed by the Leg as not sufficient to trigger the radical revision of property rights required by the shared property regime. Bastarache J, for the majority, concluded that distinction drawn by law btw legally-married spouses and CL spouses corresponded to real differences btw the rel’p and did not impair the dignity of CL spouses. The law was upheld.

55.22 Sexual orientation
Egan v Canada [1995]

227



SC held an analogous ground where the federal Old Age Security Act offended Ss. 15 by making a spousal allowance available to a spouse “of the opposite sex” BUT NOT to a same-sex partner. Provision upheld under Ss. 1 by narrow majority (5:4).

Vriend v Alberta [1998]    SC unanimously held that AB’s HR statute offended Ss. 15 by failing to provide a remedy for a person who had been discriminated by his employer on the basis of sexual orientation. Since statute provided a remedy for discrimination in employment on basis of a host of grounds including age, sex, race, religion, disability and marital status, omission of sexual orientation was denial of equal benefit of law based on an analogous ground. Omission was NOT saved under Ss. 1, b/c AB failed to adduce evidence of a legitimate legislative goal that would be advanced by the failure to protect homosexual persons from discrimination on the basis of their sexual orientation.

M v H [1999]    SC held by a majority that the exclusion of persons in same-sex relationships from the spousal support obligations in Ontario’s family law leg’n was unconstitutional. Def’n of spouse discriminated on the ground of sexual orientation (an analogous ground) AND it impaired human dignity by implying that persons in same-sex relationships were LESS worthy of protection than persons in opposite-sex rel’p. The def’n was NOT saved under Ss. 1, b/c goals of the leg’n, which were to make equitable provision for the economically weaker spouse on the breakdown of a rel’p and to ease the burden on the public purse, were NOT advanced by the exclusion of same-sex couples.

Canada v Hislop [2007]    Class action challenge to remedial law in CPP (which was changed, along with 67 other statutes in response to M v H), which changed the survivor-benefit provisions to included same-sex partners, BUT was prospective from Jan 1, 1998, so that persons whose same-sex partners had died before 1998 were ineligible for the survivors’ pension. SC held that Ss. 15 REQ eligibility for the survivors’ pension had to be made retroactive to April 15, 1985, when Ss. 15 came into force. Appropriate comparison was between survivors of opposite-sex rel’p whose partners died btw 1985 and before 1998 (and who were eligible for the pension) and survivors of same-sex rel’p whose partners died in the same period (and who were ineligible for the pension). Failure to grant eligibility to latter class was discrimination on basis of sexual orientation and could NOT be justified under Ss. 1 SC accordingly struck down the restriction on eligibility in the remedial statute. However, SC went on to make a surprising distinction btw the rule governing eligibility (which had to be retroactive) for benefits AND the rule governing payment of benefits. SC held that Parl’t could properly make payment of benefits prospective ONLY & payment provisions were upheld!

   

Little Sisters Book and Art Emporium v Canada [2000]    SC agreed that the customs officials did indeed target the imports of Little Sisters because of the homosexual content, that this treated the homosexual communities differently than the heterosexual communities and that the dignity of the importers was diminished by the differential treatment. There was a breach of Ss. 15, however, the discrimination was NOT inherent in the definition of obscenity in the customs leg’n. Discrimination occurred “at administrative level in the implementation of the customs legislation” Appropriate remedy was NOT to strike down leg’n, BUT to insist on more even & sensitive ADMIN of the leg’n.

Same-Sex Marriage Reference (Re Same-Sex Marriage) [2004]    SC held that Parlt’s power over “marriage” in Ss. 91(26) CA, 1867 extended to legalization of same-sex marriage. SC also asked to determine whether the opposite-sex requirement for marriage was consistent with the Charter. This question was the issue that was not pursued when no appeals were taken from the provincial decisions that held that the def’n of marriage breach Ss. 15 and was NOT justified under Ss. 1. The SC refused to answer this question on the ground that it would serve NO purpose, since the Gov’t was planning to introduce leg’n to eliminate the opposite-sex requirement.

228

55.23 Place of residence
R v Turpin [1989]   Not an analogous ground 3 accused who wanted trial by judge alone (allowed in AB, but in no other province).

Corbiere v Canada [1999]     Members of the Batchewana Indian Band who lived off the band’s reserve challenged the provision of the Indian Act that made residence on the reserve a REQ for voting in band elections. SC unanimously held that “Aboriginality-residence” was an analogous ground and that the voting requirement, being based on that ground, was a breach of Ss. 15 Its decision was confined to “off-reserve band member status” and did NOT apply to residence in general. “The ordinary ‘residence’ decision faced by the average Canadian should NOT be confused with the profound decisions Aboriginal band members make to live on or off their reserves, assuming choice is possible”. For aboriginals, decision to live off the reserve was often dictated by the inadequate size of the reserve, lack of housing and job opportunities on reserve and fact that some leg’n (now reversed) had removed band membership from some Indians and forced them off the reserve. Residence off the reserve was thus in many cases practically immutable in that the contrary decision would involve unacceptable personal cost. Differences in treatment of individuals that are caused by federalism MUST be able to be accommodated by the Charter.

 

R v S(S) [1990]           A provision of the federal Young Offenders Act authorized AG of each province to establish a programme of “alternative measures” to divert young offenders away from the courts. Not only did this provision contemplate that different provinces would establish different programmes, it also permitted a province to establish no programme at all. A young offender in Ontario, where there was no programme, argued that failure of Ontario to establish a programme, when 9 other provinces had done so, was a violation of his equality right under Ss. 15. Argument was rejected for the (Hogg argues wrong) reason that the discretion was not “law” (see earlier notes). SC went on to discuss constitutionality of the leg’n enabling establishment (or non-establishment) of DIFF programmes. After noting that differences btw provincial laws could NEVER amount to discrimination because of the federal principle, Dickson CJ for the Court, pointed out that province-based distinctions in federal laws could also be “a legitimate means of forwarding the values of a federal system”. This was especially so in the field of criminal justice, where “balancing of nat’l interests and local concerns has been accomplished by a constitutional structure that both permits and encourages federal-provincial co-operation”. Concluded that diversion-programmes authority in the Act was appropriate reflection of federal values, allowing as it did the adoption of diversion programmes that were suitable to the particular needs of local communities. Thus the distinction in the Act based on province of residence did NOT constitute discrimination within Ss. 15 “There can be no question, then, that unequal treatment which stems SOLELY from the exercise, by provincial legislators, of their legitimate jurisdictional powers CANNOT be the subject of a Ss. 15(1) challenge on the basis ONLY that it creates distinctions based on province of residence” per Dickson CJ (obiter dictum).

Haig v Canada [1993]   Residency requirements in voting in referenda to approve Charlottetown Accord. Haig slipped through the cracks and was NOT allowed to vote as he did not meet residency requirements. SC held decision NOT to apply Fed referendum law in ALL Prov legitimately reflected “values of a federal system”

Siemens v Manitoba [2003]    A provincial law established a local option scheme for video lottery gaming in the province. Each municipality was authorized by the law to hold a plebiscite on the banning of video lottery terminals. The provincial law gave effect to ANY such plebiscite by providing that video lottery gaming was prohibited in any municipality in which the electors had held a plebiscite and approved the ban. The law also singled out the town of Winkler, which had held such a plebiscite before the passage of the enabling law. In that plebiscite, the electors had resolved to request the province to ban video lottery terminals in Winkler.

229

  

Because the electors of Winkler had already expressed their opinion in a plebiscite, the provincial law directly prohibited video lottery gaming in Winkler. The operators of video lottery terminals in Winkler challenged this prohibition under Ss. 15. SC (citing Haig) doubted that place of residence could be an analogous ground and held that it certainly was NOT in this case, where the law simply gave effect to the majority will of the residents of the municipality

55.24 Occupation
  Occupation is NOT an analogous ground. It lacks the immutability that is common to the listed grounds and is required for the analogous grounds.

Re Workers’ Compensation Act, 1983 (Nfld) [1989]    Challenge was mounted against the provision of the Workers’ Compensation Act of Nfld that denies to an injured worker the right to sue his/her employer in tort for injuries suffered in the course of employment. It was argued that this breached Ss. 15, because other accident victims, eg, victims of road accidents, could bring a tort action against the person whose fault caused the injury. SC disposed of the case in a single paragraph, the essence of which was that the singling out of work-related accident victims was NOT based on a listed or analogous ground.

Delisle v Canada [1999]    Majority SC held that the exclusion of members of the RCMP from the federal Public Service Staff Relations Act, which regulates labour relations in the federal public service, was NOT a breach of Ss. 15 A legislative distinction btw occupational groups could NOT be challenged under Ss. 15, because occupation was NOT a “functionally immutable” characteristic that could qualify as an analogous ground. SC also reject argument based on freedom of association (Ss. 2(d)) on the ground that the police officers still had their CL right to associate even though they lacked the superior statutory rights of collective bargaining.

cf Dunmore v Ontario [2001]      SC reversed itself on the freedom of association argument and held that the exclusion of agricultural workers from Ontario’s Labour Relations Act WAS a breach of Ss. 2(d) (and could NOT be saved under Ss. 1). Hogg argues the case was not really a freedom of association case as the agricultural workers were REALLY making an equality claim. Leg’n from which they were excluded did NOT diminish their CL right to associate; what the leg’n did was to give superior rights to other workers in Ont. Bastarache J, for the maj, said that it was “not necessary” to consider Ss. 15 of the Charter, because the remedy could be had under Ss. 2(d). Major J, dissenting, pointed out that it was obvious on the basis of prior case law that the agricultural workers would NOT be able to satisfy the analogous ground requirement that had defeated the police officers in Delisle. As just noted, the mere fact of exclusion from protective legislation is not conclusive evidence of a Charter violation; as I observed in Delisle, supra, RCMP officers had the strength to form employee associations in several provinces despite their exclusion from the PSSRA (para. 31). That being said, it is possible to draw a distinction between groups who are “strong enough to look after [their] interests without collective bargaining legislation” and those “who have no recourse to protect their interests aside from the right to quit” (see Canadian Industrial Relations: The Report of the Task Force on Labour Relations (1968), at paras. 253-54). As Canada’s leading Task Force on Labour Relations recognized as early as 1968, agricultural workers fall into the latter category (para. 254). Not only have agricultural workers proved unable to form employee associations in provinces which deny them protection but, unlike the RCMP officers in Delisle, they argue that their relative status and lack of statutory protection all but guarantee this result. Distinguishing features of agricultural workers are their political impotence, their lack of resources to associate without state protection and their vulnerability to reprisal by their employers; as noted by Sharpe J., agricultural workers are “poorly paid, face difficult working conditions, have low levels of skill and education, low status and limited employment mobility” (p. 216). Moreover, unlike RCMP officers, agricultural workers are not employed by the government and therefore cannot access the Charter directly to suppress an unfair labour practice (Delisle, at para. 32). It is no wonder, therefore, according to the appellants, that agricultural workers have failed to associate in any meaningful way in Ontario, while RCMP officers have successfully created independent employee associations in several provinces across Canada (Delisle, at para. 31).

See distinction of Delisle in Dunmore at [41] per Bastarache J:

230

Health Services and Support – Facilities Subsector Bargaining Assn v BC [2001]  BC enacted Act that purported to override some rights possessed by the province’s unionized health care workers under collective agreements. The Act laid down new rules regarding seniority, layoffs, bumping and contracting out, which were designed to give the hospitals and other health-sector employers more freedom in managing their workforce in order to deliver services more efficiently and rein in the costs of publicly funded health care. Unions successfully argued that the Act was a breach of freedom of association in Ss. 2(d) SC did NOT import an equality value into Ss. 2(d), as it had done in Dunmore. INSTEAD, SC held for the first time that Ss. 2(d) directly protected the union right of collective bargaining. Because the Act substantially interfered with the terms of the collective agreements, it was invalid. The unions also made an equality argument under Ss. 15, which was based on the singling out of health care workers, and this was rejected unanimously by the SC. SC said that adverse effects of the Act on health care workers “relate essentially to the type of work they do, and NOT to the persons they are.” There was no “differential treatment based on personal characteristics”. Therefore, occupation was again rejected as an analogous ground.

     

***Withler v. Canada (Attorney General) - 2011       The appellants, who were representative plaintiffs in a class action against the Crown, were widows whose federal supplementary death benefits were reduced because of their husbands' ages at time of death. Supplementary death benefit reduced by 10% for each year by which the plan member exceeded a prescribed age. The appellants submitted that the age-based benefit reduction, which was part of a statutory death benefit scheme for certain federal government employees, violated section 15 of the Charter. While SC of Canada ultimately dismissed the widows' appeal, it rejected the Crown's argument that because their husbands were the actual subjects of the alleged discrimination, the widows lacked standing. The court granted the widows standing on the basis that as surviving spouses they suffered the alleged discriminatory effect. Furthermore, federal employees were unlikely to challenge the scheme of their own accord. With respect to the widows' substantive claim, the court emphasized that Ss 15 ONLY prohibits substantive discrimination on the grounds set out therein, OR on an analogous ground. Substantive discrimination can be made out by showing that the impugned law, in purpose or effect, perpetuates prejudice and disadvantage to members of a group on the basis of personal characteristics caught by section 15(1), or by showing that the disadvantage perpetuated by the law is based on a stereotype that does NOT correspond to the actual circumstances and characteristics of the claimant or claimant group. The court emphasized that the focus of a Ss 15 analysis is the ACTUAL impact of the differential treatment, and therefore the analysis requires a contextual consideration of the impact of the legislation or state action. After canvassing recent jurisprudence, the court cautioned against the formalistic use of comparator groups as part of this contextual assessment!! The court noted that the probative value of a comparative analysis varies depending on nature of the claim and, as a result, such an analysis is NOT always required in considering whether substantive discrimination is made out. In the claim at issue, the court concluded that the focus MUST be on the nature of the benefit. A contextual assessment revealed that the age-based benefit reduction did NOT breach Ss 15. The scheme was designed to benefit a number of different groups, and the benefit reductions reflected the reality that different groups of survivors have different needs. In support of its conclusion, the court noted that the impugned benefit was NOT meant to provide a long-term income scheme for older surviving spouses, as such a scheme is provided by a distinct pension benefit. The court explicitly rejected the dissenting opinion of Rowles J.A. of the BC CoA, whose narrower comparative analysis failed to consider the impugned benefit reductions within the context of the entire package of available benefits. Having found Ss 15(1) was not breached by benefit scheme, the court did NOT perform a section 1 analysis. This decision is part of a trend, reflected in earlier decisions such as Kapp, toward a MORE contextual analysis of the existence of substantive discrimination in assessing whether a legislative scheme is discriminatory. Held that elements of pension benefit schemes must be considered in the context of the scheme as a whole. The SDB was thus viewed as one part of a broad-based scheme addressing the competing interests of employees at different stages of their lives. The SCC described the SDB’s purpose with respect to older employees as financial assistance for last illness and death, and not as a long-term income stream  DIFFERENT CONTEXT OF HOW BENEFIT IS TO APPLY THUS JUSTIFYING DISTINCTION BASED ON AGE

    

  

231

CHAPTER 40: ENFORCEMENT OF RIGHTS

40.1 Supremacy clause
(a) Section 52(1)  Section 52(1) of the CA, 1982 provides: Primacy of Constitution of Canada 52. (1) CC is supreme law of Canada, and any law that is inconsistent w/ provisions of CC is, to extent of the inconsistency, of no force or effect.   Since the Charter is part of the “CC”, any law that is inconsistent with the Charter is “of no force or effect”. Effect of the supremacy clause (Ss. 52(1)) is to preserve all pre-existing remedies for unconstitutional action and to extend those remedies to the Charter of Rights.

(b) Section 24(1) compared  In addition to Ss. 52(1), the Charter contains its own remedy clause. Ss. 24 (1) provides: Enforcement of guaranteed rights and freedoms 24. (1) Anyone whose rights or freedoms, as guaranteed by this Charter, have been infringed or denied may apply to a court of competent jurisdiction to obtain such remedy as the court considers appropriate and just in the circumstances.  The differences btw Ss. 24(1) and Ss. 52(1) are as follows: 1. Ss. 24(1) is applicable ONLY to breaches of the Charter; Ss. 52(1) is applicable to the entire Constitution, including the Charter; 2. Ss. 24(1) is available ONLY to a person whose rights have been infringed; s 52(1) is available in some circumstances to persons whose rights have not been infringed; 3. Ss. 24(1) may be applied ONLY by a “court of competent jurisdiction”; s 52(1) may be applied by ANY court or tribunal with power to decide questions of law 4. Ss. 24(1) authorizes the award of a wide range of remedies; s 52(1) appears to authorize ONLY a holding of invalidity, leaving it to the general law to authorize the particular remedy 5. Ss. 24(1) confers a discretion on the court as to whether any remedy should be awarded; s 52(1) appears to confer NO discretion on the court, requiring the court to make a holding of invalidity if it concludes that a law or act is inconsistent with the Constitution. Although Ss. 52(1) appears to impose no discretion on remedies, the courts have developed a number of variations on simple declaration of invalidity & assumed the power to choose from a range of possible remedies: 1. Nullification, ie, striking down (declaring invalid) the statute that is inconsistent with the CC; 2. Temporary validity, striking down BUT temp suspending coming into force of declaration of invalidity; 3. Severance, ie, holding only part of the statute is inconsistent with the CC, striking down only that part and severing it form the valid remainder; 4. Reading in, ie, adding words to a statute that is inconsistent with the CC so as to make the statute consistent with the CC and valid; 5. Reading down, ie, interpreting a statute that could be interpreted as inconsistent with the CC so that it is consistent; 6. Constitutional exemption, ie, creating exemption from a statute that is partly inconsistent with the Constitution so as to exclude from the statute the application that would be inconsistent with the CC. IF law is held invalid, effect of holding is litigation will be determined as if unconstitutional law did NOT exist. IF the litigation is a criminal prosecution, the person charged under the invalid law will be entitled to be acquitted. IF the litigation is a civil action, the party relying on the invalid law will lose the legal basis for his or her case.



(c) Nullification   

232

(d) Temporary validity Re Manitoba Language Rights [1985]    SC assumed the power to hold that unconstitutional laws were to be given “temporary force and effect” to allow the Leg time to enact the required corrective leg’n. Manitoba’s Leg failed to enact laws in French & English, which had invalidated the entire Manitoba statute book. Court invoked the “rule of law” to keep the unconstitutional laws temporarily in force in order to avoid a vacuum of law in the province. (Hogg argues necessity better argument)

SC has employed this tactic in the following non-Charter cases: o o R v Mercure [1988] (Sask); R v Paquette [1990] (Alta) – laws enacted in English only in Sask and AB Sinclair v Que [1992] – by-laws and other acts of a Quebec municipality that had been invalidly incorporated, but which had been functioning for 6 years.

And in the following Charter cases: o R v Brydges [1990] – Lamer J for maj, after deciding that the police were under a constitutional duty to advise ALL persons under arrest of the availability of legal aid, allowed a “transition period” of 30 days to enable the police forces to prepare new cautions R v Askov [1990] – Cory J for the maj, after deciding that the District of Peel in Ontario had unreasonable systemic delay in its criminal court process, raised the possibility of “a transitional period of lenient treatment of systemic delay”. Ultimately, did not do so, which cause 1000s of charges to be stayed over the next few months. R v Feeney [1997] – SC held, by virtue of Ss. 8 of the Charter, a warrant was required to enter a dwelling house in order to make a lawful arrest. This changed CL. CC made NO provision for the issue of the entry warrant, BUT Sopinka J, for the maj, held that the provision should be “read in”. After the dec’n, the Crown applied for a stay of the req of the entry warrant for 6 month; stay granted, but expressed so as not to affect the disposition of the Feeney case.

o

o

The above cases did not involve unconstitutional legislation, BUT there is now a series of cases in which courts have maintained leg’n in force after holding it to be invalid for breach of the Charter. Dixon v BC (1989) (BCSC)     Held, provincial leg’n prescribing electoral districts for the province was unconstitutional; the disparity btw the voting populations of different districts was so great as to violate Ss. 3 of the Charter (right to vote). McLachlin CJ (CJ of BCSC) pointed out that the nullification of the leg’n would leave the province without the means of holding an election and in a system of responsible gov’t it was possible for an election to be called at any time. This qualified as “an emergency” comparable to that recognized in Re Manitoba Language Rights and a similar order was required. She held that the unconstitutional leg’n “will stay provisionally in place to avoid the constitutional crisis which would occur should a precipitate election be required”

R v Swain [1991] o o o o Maj SC struck down the provisions of the CCd that required the detention in a psychiatric facility of a person acquitted on the ground of insanity (breach of Ss. 7 and 9 of the Charter) The invalidity of the provisions meant that a person acquitted on the ground of insanity would simply be released like any other accused who had been found not guilty at trial. Since a person acquitted on ground of insanity has usually committed serious offence and may well be a continued danger to the public, his/her release into the community would often involve serious risks. SC held that there was to be a 6 month “period of temporary validity” so that judges would not be compelled to release into the community all insanity acquittees

233

R v Bain [1992]    Maj SC struck down the provisions of the CCd that allowed the Crown prosecutor, but NOT the accused, to “stand by” prospective jurors. The provisions were held to be contrary to the guarantee of a fair trial in Ss. 11(d) of the Charter, because they gave the Crown MORE influence than the accused in the selection of the jury. Here, the dec’n of invalidity was suspended for 6 months in order to “provide an opportunity for Parliament to remedy the situation if it considers it appropriate to do so”

Schachter v Canada [1992]         SC held that provision of Fed Unemployment Insurance Act offended the guarantee of equality in Ss. 15 of the Charter, B/C provision allowed MORE generous child care benefits to adoptive parents than to natural parents. This meant that the statute was “under-inclusive”, meaning that it failed to include a class of people (here, natural parents) that had an equality-based constitutional right to be included. Lamer CJ for the maj pointed out that striking down under-inclusive statute would have the perverse result of denying statutory benefits to deserving class covered by the statute (adoptive parents in this case), while granting no benefit to the class that was unconstitutionally excluded (natural parents in this case) He held that: “The logical remedy is to strike down BUT suspend declaration of invalidity to allow the gov’t to determine whether to cancel OR extend the benefits” Parl’t had by then amended Act, SC held that there was “no need for a declaration of invalidity or a suspension thereof”. SC granted no remedy at all to the unfortunate P, despite his success on the substantive issue of equality rights. However, the SC did order the Crown to pay his costs. Lamer CJ did recognize the radical character of a declaration of invalidity, saying it is a “serious interference” w/ legislative process, b/c delayed nullification “forces the matter back onto the legislative agenda at a time not of choosing of legislature and w/n time limits under which the legislature would not normally be forced to act”. He said that it was preferable for the Court to rectify the statute by severance or reading in. Where declaration of validity was to be made, a temporary suspension of the declaration should ONLY be made where the immediate striking down of the leg’n: 1. “would pose a danger to the public” (as in Swain); 2. “would threaten the rule of law” (as in the Manitoba Language case); or 3. “would result in the deprivation of benefits from deserving persons” (as in Schachter itself).

These guidelines have largely been ignored in subsequent cases, because a new rationale of “dialogue” has developed for the suspended declaration of invalidity. Examples of suspension of declaration of invalidity: o o o o o o o o Re Eurig Estate [1998], SC invalidated Ont’s probate fees; ONLY reason cited for suspension of 6 months was the potential loss of revenue to the province Corbiere v Canada [1999], SC invalidated on-reserve residence requirements in Indian Act for Indian band elections; suspension 18 months to enable new provisions drafted after consultation w/ Aboriginal people UFCW v Kmart Canada [1999], SC invalidated an overbroad prohibition of secondary picketing; suspension for 6 months to allow time to draft new provisions Dunmore v Ontario [2001], SC invalidated provision excluding agricultural workers from Ontario’s labour relations statute; suspended for 18 months to allow time for amending leg’n to be drafted and enacted R v Guignard [2002], SC invalidated a by-law restricting advertising signs in the municipality; suspended for 6 months Trociuk v BC [2003], SC invalidated powers given to a mother but not a father in a provincial birth-registration law; suspended for 12 months Figueroa v Canada [2003], SC invalidated restrictions that disqualified small political parties from registration for purpose of federal elections; suspended for 12 months “in order to enable gov’t to comply w/ reasons” Health Services Bargaining case; SC invalidated a statute that increased health-care employers’ right to manage their unionized workforce; suspended for 12 months “to allow the gov’t to address the repercussions of this decision” Confédération des syndicates nationaux v Canada [2008], SC held that the employment insurance premiums levied during a 3-year period were invalid; suspended for 12 months “to allow the consequences of that invalidity to be rectified”

o

234

  

A suspended declaration of invalidity is NOT to be confused with a prospective ruling. A suspended declaration of invalidity is delayed in coming into force, but if and when it comes into force it has the normal retroactive effect of a court order. Operates to invalidate unconstitutional statute from time of its enactment. If corrected by legislation (declaration won’t come into force at all), therefore, would think that the leg’n should be retroactive to give a remedy to litigants.

This was overlooked by the SC in: Canada v Hislop [2007]   Maj SC said that “the purpose of a suspended declaration of invalidity can be to facilitate the legislature’s function in crafting a prospective remedy” SC upheld a corrective statute that conferred survivor-pension rights on same-sex couples, but for the future ONLY, thus denying the claimants the past benefits to which they would have been entitled had the pension leg’n been enacted in compliance with Ss. 15 (equality).

(e) Severance o o o Severance is the appropriate remedy when only part of the statute is held to be invalid and the rest can independently survive. Eg, the invalidity of the search and seizure power of the Competition Act does NOT entail the striking down of the entire Act: Hunter v Southam [1984] Eg, the invalidity of the felony-murder rule of the CCd does NOT entail the striking down of the entire CCd: R v Vaillancourt [1987]

R v Hess [1990]     CCd provision made it an offence for a male person to have intercourse with a female person under 14 yrs of age, “whether or not he believes that she is fourteen years of age or more”. The quoted language caused the offence to violate Ss. 7, because it eliminated the requirement of mens rea for an essential element of the offence, ie, the age of the girl. The constitutional problem could be corrected by excising the quoted words. The Court accordingly invoked the power of severance to strike out the unconstitutional words. The result was a significant change in the statutory provision, but the alternative would have been to strike down the entire offence, which would mean that intercourse with a girl under 14 would no longer be an offence, even for a man who was aware of the girl’s age.

Tétreault-Gadoury v Canada [1991] o o o o o o There was an age-65 bar in the Unemployment Insurance Act, which took the form of an exception to the general rules of entitlement. SC held this requirement was a breach of Ss. 15 IF the age bar were removed from the Act, the normal rules of entitlement would operate without any limitation as to age. Accordingly, the SC severed the age-65 bar from the Act. The effect was to require payment of unemployment insurance benefits to persons over 65. Here, the statutory scheme was “under-inclusive”, meaning that the scheme failed to include a class of people (those over 65) who had a constitutional right to be included. In such a case, the obvious remedy is to hold the statute unconstitutional, but this produces the harsh result of denying unemployment insurance benefits to persons under 65. Another alternative is to “extend” the statutory benefits to persons over 65 by striking out the words that made the statutory scheme under-inclusive. (Further alternative is a suspended declaration, as in Schachter) The remedy of extension directly alters the statutory scheme and requires new expenditures by the federal gov’t that have need been authorized by Parl’t. Thus a court faced with an under-inclusive statute has an unpalatable choice. IN choosing remedy of extension, Court in Tétreault-Gadoury influenced by the fact that by the time the case was decided Parl’t had repealed the age-bar (although not with retroactive effect so as to entitle the applicant)

o o o o

235

o

La Forest J for the Court said “there was no evidence put forth that the government could not afford to extend benefits to those over sixty-five” [with no supporting evidence]

Benner v Canada [1997]      Breach of Ss. 15 – Citizenship Act – drew distinction btw children of Canadian mothers born abroad before 1977 and fathers of such children. Mother – child had to apply for citizenship and pass a security test; father – child automatically entitled to citizenship on registration of birth in Canada SC could have imposed the more stringent mother-derived rule or the more liberal father-derived rule. SC chose father-derived automatic right, because the automatic right of citizenship was already the rule for children born abroad to Canadian parent of either sex after 1977. Therefore, the legislative scheme would be less disturbed by the extension of the automatic right of citizenship. The SC achieved that result be severing a number of phrases from the Act.

(f) Reading in Schacter v Canada (1992) o o o Leg’n more generous to adoptive parents. There was no severable provision excluding natural parents from the child care benefits. SC held that it possessed the power not only to sever language, but ALSO to “read in” new language IF that were necessary to remedy a constitutional defect. The defect in Schachter could be cured by “reading in” the class of natural parents to the statutory provision benefiting adoptive parents. Lamer CJ, for unanimous SC on this issue, said that reading in would be a “legitimate remedy akin to severance” He said that it seems wrong that the “style of drafting” of a statute should be “the single critical factor in the determination of a remedy” However, Lamer CJ said that reading in would be appropriate ONLY in “the clearest of cases”, such as cases where: 1. 2. 3. 4.      the addition of the excluded class was consistent with the legislative objective; there seemed to be little choice as to how to cure the constitutional defect; the reading in would not involve a substantial change in the cost or nature of the legislative scheme; and the alternative of striking down the under-inclusive provision would be an inferior remedy

o o o

SC held that reading in was NOT appropriate. The objective of the Act in making special provision for adoptive parents was NOT clear and it could NOT be assumed that the addition of natural parents to the provision would be consistent with the legislative objective. Furthermore, the reading in of natural parents would cause a major increase in the scope and cost of the child care benefits legislated by Parl’t. SC concluded “to read in natural parents would in these circumstances constitute a substantial intrusion into legislative domain” SC held that appropriate remedy was a suspended declaration of invalidity

Miron v Trudel [1995] o o o o SC held that Ont’s Insurance Act, which stipulated terms of compulsory automobile insurance policies in province, contained an unconstitutional provision for accident benefit that was payable to “spouse” of an insured person. Def’n of spouse restricted to those legally married; SC held discriminated against claimant, who was a CL spouse of an insured person, on the basis of marital status. SC held that the appropriate remedy for the defect was to read into the def’n of spouse NEW language that would include the unconstitutionally-excluded class of CL spouses. Leg had already amended the leg’n, so the SC ordered that the language of the subsequent amendment be made retroactive to cover the claimant’s claim of spousal status

236

*** Vriend v Alberta [1998]      Alberta’s Individual Rights Protection Act prohibited discrimination in employment, accommodation, public facilities and the supply of goods and services. P had been discharged from employment by reason of homosexuality. Attempt to seek remedy under Act from AB’s HR Commission unsuccessful b/c Act, while prohibiting discrimination on a variety of grounds, did NOT cover discrimination based on sexual orientation. SC agreed w/ P that omission of sexual orientation from Act denied P’s equality rights under the Charter. SC ordered that the constitutional defect be cured by reading into the statutory list of grounds of prohibited discrimination the words “sexual orientation”. According to Iacobucci J, for the maj, the addition of this language to the Act would be consistent with the objective of the Act, could be accomplished with precision, would not greatly add to the cost of administering the Act and would be a less intrusive remedy than striking down the entire Act. [Schacter Criteria]

Miron and Vriend are BOTH examples of the remedy of “extension”. In these cases, as in Tétreault-Gadoury, the SC had to review an “under-inclusive” statute, ie, a statute that conferred a benefit on a class that failed to include all persons who had an equality-based right to be included. In Tétreault-Gadoury, however, the extension could be accomplished by severing words rather than reading in, as was necessary in Miron and Vriend. Reading in and severance are obviously intrusive, BUT the alternative of striking down the scheme is ALSO intrusive. Nova Scotia v Phillips [(1986) (NSCA) –  NS CoA held that a welfare benefit that was available only to single mothers was in breach of Ss. 15, because it was NOT available to single fathers as well.  Being unwilling to extend the statutory provision by reading in the excluded group of single fathers, the Court was driven to nullify the provision, which denied the benefit to single mothers!!!  This restored equality, but it was obviously an ironic and harsh result. Another option is suspended declaration, which is also a major intrusion into the legislative branch. Nevertheless, whatever remedy is chosen, if the legislators are not content with the scheme as amended by the court, it can always enact a new scheme. The democratic legislative process retains the last word. (g) Reading down    Appropriate remedy when statute will bear 2 interpretations, one of which would offend Charter and the other not. In that case, a court will hold that the latter interpretation, which is normally the narrower one, is the correct one. CONTRASTED w/ “Reading in” – reading in is NOT a technique of interpretation, but rather of judicial amendment, altering the statute to make it conform to the Constitution.

(h) Constitutional exemption R v Big M Drug Mart [1985]; R v Edwards Books and Art [1986]  SC has occasionally indicated, obiter, that it might be willing to grant a “constitutional exemption” form “otherwise valid legislation” that would be unconstitutional in its application to particular individuals or groups  Sunday closing, this would seem to indicate that a prohibition of retailing on a Sunday COULD BE salvaged by reading into the legislation an exemption for those who observed some day other than Sunday as the Sabbath.

237

R v Seaboyer [1991]       Rape shield provision of CCd – restricting evidence that accused can lead of the complainant’s past sexual activity Maj SC held that in some situations the rape-shield section would deprive an accused of the right to make full answer and defence. SC held that this made the section unconstitutional and McLachlin J, for the maj, refused to salvage the section through the doctrine of constitutional exemption. The exemption would operate by importing into the section a discretionary power of dispensation by the TJ. However, since Parl’t deliberately chose to exclude any discretion on the part of the TJ, the importation into the section of judicial discretion, “while perhaps saving the law in one sense, dramatically alters it in another”. McLachlin CJ did make clear that the solution “May be appropriate in some other case” Possibility of using solution for mandatory minimum sentences was definitively rejected by a unanimous SC in R v Ferguson [2008]; instead the SC strikes down the provision if in the rare hypothetic case it would be cruel and unusual: R v Smith [1987]

R v Ferguson (2008) o o o o o o o D was a police officer who was attacked by a violent prisoner whom he was trying to put into a cell. In the course of the struggle that ensued, the D’s gun went off and killed the prisoner. The D was acquitted of murder, BUT convicted of manslaughter. CCd imposed a minimum sentence of 4 years for manslaughter with a firearm. However, the TJ took the view that the sentence was grossly disproportionate punishment on these facts. W/o striking down the minimum sentence, the TJ held that the D was entitled to a constitutional exemption from the sentence and he imposed a sentence of 2 years less a day. SC disagreed, holding that there was “no basis” for concluding that the 4-year sentence was grossly disproportionate “on the facts of this case” As a result there was no need to consider the availability of a constitutional exemption. However, the SC went on to do so anyway and held that a constitutional exemption from a minimum sentence was NOT a remedy that was available to a D in a rare, exceptional or unusual case where the minimum sentence would be grossly disproportionate. The remedy in such a case was to strike down the law in its entirety. SC affirmed Smith that, if a minimum-sentence law is grossly disproportionate in any of its applications, however rare, exceptional or unusual, the duty of the sentencing court is to strike down the law. The overbroad law CANNOT be salvaged by creating a constitutional exemption for the particular rare case. The effect of creating an exemption would be “to read in a discretion to a provision where Parl’t clearly intended to exclude discretion” and this would be “an inappropriate intrusion into the legislative sphere” McLachlin CJ was also concerned that ROL values of “certainty, accessibility, intelligibility, clarity and predictability” would be offended if the minimum sentence remained in the statute book, but was in fact subject to discretionary case-by-case exceptions: as constitutional exemptions were granted, the law in the statute book would “increasingly diverge from the law as applied”

o

o o o

(i) Reconstruction  There is a point at which a court will recognize that an unconstitutional statute CANNOT be salvaged except by changes that are too profound, too policy-laden and too controversial to be carried out by a court.

Hunter v Southam [1984]    SC held that the search and seizure power in the Competition Act was unconstitutional under Ss. 8 of the Charter, b/c the Act did NOT stipulate a standard of reasonable and probable cause for the issue of a search warrant and did NOT invest an impartial judicial body with the power to issue the warrant. Dickson J, for the Court, refused to salvage the provision by reading in the required standards; he asserted that “it is the legislature’s responsibility to enact legislation that embodies appropriate safeguards to comply with the CC’s requirements” SC therefore struck down the statutory provision, leaving Parliament to enact an amendment that would satisfy the constitutional requirements.

238

Singh v Minister of Emplt & Immgt [1985]        SC held that the refugee-determination provisions of the Immigration Act were unconstitutional, because they did not provide for a hearing by an official with power to decide. Beetz J said there was “probably more than one way to remedy the constitutional shortcomings of the Immigration Act” and it was “not the function of this Court to re-write the Act”. There were occasions when “crude surgery” could be performed by the Court, “but not plastic or re-constructive surgery” SC struck down the refugee-determination procedure, leaving it to Parl’t to come up with new provisions. SC held that a prohibition on advertising by dentists, w/ some exceptions, was a breach of freedom of expression and was not saved by Ss. 1 SC made clear that SOME restrictions on professional advertising would be saved by Ss. 1, but it still struck the prohibition down in its entirety. McLachlin J said that it was “for legislators” to determine what kinds of advertising should be prohibited

Rocket v Royal College of Dental Surgeons [1990]

Canadian Foundation for Children, Youth and the Law v Canada [2004]         Section 43 CCd – defence to assault charge for teachers and parents who apply “reasonable” force “by way of correction” against children in their charge. Challenged based on void for vagueness doctrine of Ss. 7 McLachlin CJ, for the maj, issued a new “interpretation” of the provision. Ignoring the lower-court decisions to the contrary, she held that the provision “exempts from criminal sanction ONLY minor corrective force of a transitory and trifling nature” This excluded ALL “harmful conduct”, which meant that the provision did NOT apply to corporal punishment of children under two or corporal punishment of teenagers! It did NOT apply to discipline by the use of objects such as belts or rulers or by blows or slaps to the head. So interpreted, she held that the provision established a sufficiently clear standard to avoid the charge of unconstitutional vagueness. Arbour J, dissenting, objected that the CJ was not engaged in “mere interpretation”, but had drafted “an entirely new provision”

Montreal v 2952-1366 Quebec [2005]      A city by-law prohibited noise produced by sound equipment that could be heard outside a building. The by-law included no stipulations as to the level of noise or the impact on neighbours or passers-by. The by-law was a limit on freedom of expression, BUT the majority upheld it under Ss. 1 by interpreting the by-law as applying ONLY to “noise that adversely affects the enjoyment of the environment” Binnie J, dissenting, pointed out that the by-law by its terms purported to prohibit “any audible signal from ‘sound equipment’ without regard to the potential, if any, for disturbance or annoyance” and he would have held that this was too broad to be a reasonable limit under Ss. 1 He described the majority’s “interpretation” of the by-law as “radical surgery” which had left the realm of interpretation and entered the realm of amendment He said that it would be “More respectful of our place in the constitutional scheme” to declare the by-law invalid and “send it back to the legislators for consideration and possible re-enactment”

The SC will re-draft where the offending law is a rule of the CL. R v Swain [1991]     CL rule allowed a Crown prosecutor in a criminal trial to adduce evidence of the accused’s insanity AGAINST the wish of the accused. SC held this rule violated Ss. 7 of the Charter, because the evidence of insanity, IF believed, would limit the right of the accused to conduct his/her own defence. SC held that the rule would be saved by Ss. 1 IF the issue of insanity were raised by the Crown in a separate hearing, in front of the same trier of fact, which would take place after the conclusion of the criminal trial and which would ONLY take place if the accused had been found otherwise guilty of the offence charged. SC held that it was NOT nec to strike down the existing law. B/c existing law was a rule of the judge-made CL, not statute, the SC had the power to replace the existing rule with a new rule that complied with the Charter.

239

(j) Limitation of actions    An action or other form of proceeding for a declaration that a statute is unconstitutional is NOT subject to any limitation period. IF the reviewing court holds that the statute is unconstitutional, THEN the statute will be declared to have been unconstitutional from its inception and the newly discovered rights and obligations that flow from the retroactive disappearance of the statute will take effect automatically. If the P needs additional relief, such as damages or an injunction, those “personal remedies”, although based on a constitutional ground, will be subject to any applicable statute of limitations of general application.

40.2 Remedy clause
(a) Section 24(1) Enforcement of guaranteed rights and freedoms 24. (1) Anyone whose rights or freedoms, as guaranteed by this Charter, have been infringed or denied may apply to a court of competent jurisdiction to obtain such remedy as the court considers appropriate and just in the circumstances. (b) Applicable to Charter ONLY   Ss 24 is NOT a remedy for unconstitutional action in general, which may ONLY be challenged by methods referred to in the discussion of the supremacy clause. A Charter right is: o Part of “the supreme law of Canada” to which inconsistent laws MUST yield (Ss. 52(1)); o It becomes entrenched (alterable ONLY by the process of constitutional amendment) (Ss. 52(3)); and o It becomes enforceable under Ss. 24

(c) Non-exclusive remedy o o Ss 24(1) is NOT exclusive remedy for breach of the Charter; the supremacy clause of Ss. 52(1) is ALSO available. Whenever the validity of a statute (or other official act) is relevant to the outcome of a dispute, the court that is seized of the dispute has the power and the duty to determine whether the statute (or other act) is valid. o Eg, a court trying a criminal case is obliged to rule on the validity of the statute creating the offence if the D argues that the statute violates the Charter; if the court accepts the D’s argument, THEN the court will acquit the defendant on the ground that the offence charged is NOT known to the law Here, the ruling of invalidity is authorized by Ss. 52(1) and the remedy of acquittal is authorized by the CCd. Section 24(1) is NOT needed in such a case and it is not applicable. Section 24(1) is not needed to provide a remedy for a Charter infringement where a holding of invalidity is all that the applicant needs in order to obtain an appropriate remedy that is available under the general law. Section 24(1) is needed ONLY where a remedy provided by the general law is NOT available for some reason or will not provide satisfactory redress. Generally speaking, it will be the declaration of invalidity under Ss. 52(1) that provides the remedy for laws that violate a Charter right (or indeed any constitutional right), while Ss. 24(1) provides the remedy for gov’t acts that violate an individual’s Charter right.

o o o o

R v Ferguson (2008)  Ss 24(1) provides a “personal remedy against unconstitutional government action”. That is why the only person who has standing to seek a remedy under Ss. 24(1) is the very person whose Charter right has been infringed. R v Demers [2004] o o o o BOTH remedies were needed to give full relief to the claimant. SC held that the provisions of the CCd respecting accused person who were found permanently unfit to stand trial were unconstitutional for overbreadth. The SC suspended the declaration of invalidity for a period of one year to permit Parl’t to enact a new law. That, of course, was a remedy under Ss. 52(1) BUT the SC was concerned that, IF Parl’t failed to act, persons unfit to stand trial would STILL be subject to criminal charges and to the restrictions on their liberty that follow from criminal charges

240

o

SC therefore added a 2nd remedy. Acting under Ss. 24(1), the SC ordered that, in the event of Parl’t not acting within a year, those persons permanently unfit to stand trial who were found to be no danger to the public should have their proceedings stayed.

(d) Standing  Section 24(1) contemplates that it is the applicant’s own rights that have been infringed or denied. R v Edwards [1996]  SC held that an accused could not complain about unreasonable search GF’s apartment, even though the search yielded evidence (a cache of drugs) that was relied upon by the Crown as part of its case against the accused.  Since the accused had NO reasonable expectation of privacy in someone else’s apartment, the search was NOT a breach of the accused’s Ss. 8 right to be secure against unreasonable search or seizure.  ONLY the tenant of the apartment could pursue a remedy under Ss. 24 R v Big M Drug Mart [1985]    A corporation CANNOT obtain a remedy under Ss. 24(1) for a denial of freedom of religion, because a corporation CANNOT hold a religious belief. BUT, Big M (a corp) COULD defend a criminal charge under a Sunday closing law on the ground that the law was a denial of the freedom of religion of individuals. A D to a criminal charge was at liberty to raise any constitutional defect in the law under which the charge is laid, b/c “no one can be convicted of an offence under an unconstitutional law’. However, the corp had NO standing under Ss. 24(1) to challenge law. BUT, this was irrelevant. The challenge was based on supremacy clause of Ss. 52(1): “Where, as here, the challenge is based on the unconstitutionality of legislation, recourse to Ss. 24 is unnecessary and the particular effect on the challenged party is irrelevant”

Minister of Justice v Borowski [1981]      Sometimes a person, motivated by public interest, wishes to make a Charter challenge to a statute that does not even apply to the challenger. This CANNOT be done under Ss. 24(1) HOWEVER, it CAN be done under Ss. 52(1) SC granted standing to an anti-abortion activist to bring an action for a declaration that the CCd’s abortion provisions were unconstitutional. Those provisions could NEVER actually be applied to the applicant, who was neither a doctor nor a woman, but he was granted standing nevertheless. This illustrates that the availability of a declaration of invalidity under Ss. 52(1) is governed by more generous standing requirements than are the remedies authorized by Ss. 24(1)

(e) Apprehended infringements o o Section 24(1) does NOT authorize an application in respect of a merely apprehended future infringement. However, the imminent threat of a Charter violation WILL satisfy Ss. 24(1)

Que Ass of Protestant School Bds v AG Que (1982) (Que SC) (decn affirmed in CA and SC, BUT no comment on remedial issue) o Ss. 24(1) will authorize a remedy for English-speaking parents who are denied by statute their Charter right under Ss. 23 to send their children to an English-speaking school, EVEN IF the application is made before the school year has started and therefore before any parent’s child has actually been refused admission o It is unclear whether Ss. 24(1) will extend as far as the general remedies of declaration and injunction, which are both available in respect of threatened violations of rights. R v Demers (2004)    Suspended declaration of invalidity – CCd provisions dealing with accused persons who are unfit to stand trial. Second remedy – IF Parl’t did NOT act, persons who were permanently unfit to stand trial and who had been found NOT to be a danger to the public should receive a stay of proceedings. SC obviously feared that the infringement might occur in the future.

241

(f) Court of competent jurisdiction o o Section 24(1)’s remedies may be granted ONLY by a “court of competent jurisdiction” o Ss. 52 finding of invalidity is available to ANY court or tribunal with power to decide questions of law. The meaning of “court of competent jurisdiction” is relevant ONLY to the availability of a remedy under Ss. 24(1)

R v Rahey [1987]; R v Smith [1989] o o o A superior court, which is a court of general jurisdiction, is ALWAYS a court of competent jurisdiction An application for a remedy under Ss. 24(1), then, can ALWAYS be made to a superior court. Even after trial commenced, an application to a superior court may be appropriate if it is the action of the TJ that is the subject of the complaint; eg, when the TJ has failed to render a decision on a motion for a directed verdict

Doucet-Boudreau v Nova Scotia [2003] o Moreover, the power of a superior court to grant a remedy under Ss. 24(1) CANNOT be limited by statute However, if, eg, an applicant interrupted a trial in an inferior court in order to bring a Ss. 24(1) application before a superior court, the superior court should normally refuse the application on the ground that the trial court is the most convenient forum to hear the Ss. 24(1) application. R v 974649 [2001] o A trial court, even if it is not a superior court, is a court of competent jurisdiction to hear an application for a remedy that relates to the conduct of the trial, eg, the exclusion of evidence that has been obtained in violation of the Charter or a stay of proceedings that have gone on for an unreasonable time or an award of costs against the Crown for failure to make timely disclosure to the defence o If a separate application were made to a superior court, the superior court should generally decline to grant any remedy, because it is the TJ who is in the best position to assess what is the remedy that is just and appropriate o However, a D to a criminal charge who has not been given a trial date, and is not likely to get one for a long time, has no trial court within reach and therefore may apply to a superior court to stay the proceedings on the ground of unreasonable delay R v Hynes [2001]  SC held that judge conducting a preliminary inquiry into a criminal charge is NOT a court of competent jurisdiction R v Smith [1989] o o This means that a preliminary inquiry judge has no power to stay proceedings on the ground of unreasonable delay under Ss. 11(b) and NO power to exclude evidence on the ground that the evidence was obtained in breach of the Charter In the case where the D claims that the trial of a criminal charge has been unreasonably delayed because too much time has elapsed even before the prelim has been held, the D can make a separate application to a superior court to stay the proceedings

An administrative tribunal is a court of competent jurisdiction IF its constituent state gave it power over: 1. 2. the parties to the dispute; the subject matter of the dispute; and

3. the Charter remedy that was sought: Weber v Ont Hydro [1995] The above proposition was reviewed in the following case because there was some inconsistency with tribunal’s authority under Ss. 52: R v Conway [2010]   To reconcile the differences/inconsistencies, the SC held that an administrative tribunal with authority to decide questions of law was a court of competent jurisdiction for the purpose of Ss. 24 (UNLESS Charter issues had been clearly withdrawn from the tribunal’s jurisdiction). It was the authority to decide questions of law that qualified an administrative tribunal to decide constitutional questions, whether those questions arose under Ss. 24 or Ss. 52

242

   

Here, the applicant had in 1984 been charged with a crime and found not guilty by reason of insanity. The CCd required that he be incarcerated in mental health facilities subject to regular reviews of his case by the Ontario Review Board, which was an administrative tribunal established by the CCd for the province of Ontario with supervisory jurisdiction over NCR patients. The Board held an annual hearing for each patient. In 2006, in advance of his annual review by the Board, the applicant applied to the Board under Ss. 24 of the Charter for an absolute discharge from custody on the ground that his Charter rights had been infringed. The application was initially denied by the Board (and on appeal by the Ont CA), using the 3 part test from Weber: the Board lacked the power to grant the remedy sought.

        

The SC, applying its new test, that the Board was a court of competent jurisdiction because it was authorized by the CCd to decide questions of law. However, the Court agreed that the Board lacked the power to grant the remedy sought and therefore dismissed the applicant’s appeal. Once it is determined that an administrative tribunal is a court of competent jurisdiction, the remaining question is whether the tribunal can grant the remedy sought. SC held that proper approach for administrative tribunals & inferior courts is to confine it to its normal range of remedies (rather than “such remedy as court considers appropriate and just in the circumstances”: Ss. 24(1)), so that Charter claim does NOT move it beyond its statutory mandate, which alone is sphere of its undoubted competence. The tribunal did NOT need to have explicit statutory authority over the precise remedy sought, but the tribunal was restricted to “the kind of remedy that legislature intended would fit w/n statutory framework of particular tribunal”. In this case, the CCd imposed some restrictions on the remedial powers of a Review Board. In particular, Board was explicitly prohibited from granting an absolute discharge to a NCR patient who had been found by the Board to be “a significant threat to the safety of the public”. The Board had found that the applicant WAS a significant threat to the safety of the public and therefore the Board had NO statutory power to grant an absolute discharge. The applicant argued that Ss. 24 freed the Board from statutory limits to its jurisdiction, BUT the Court disagreed. The withdrawal of the power to grant an absolute discharge to NCR patient who was dangerous was consistent with the Board’s mandates to protect public safety and to adequately prepare NCR patients for their reintegration into society. For the Board to grant such a remedy under Ss. 24 “would be a clear contradiction of Parliament’s intent”. The application for an absolute discharge had to be denied.

(g) Range of remedies        Limited by phrase “such remedy as the court considers appropriate and just in the circumstances”. The courts have usually insisted that existing procedures be employed for Ss. 24(1) applications, so that well-understood procedural values are not ignored simply because a constitutional point is in issue. Eg, applications respecting the conduct of a trial should normally be made to the TJ, who is in the best position to determine whether a trial should be delayed or interrupted. Subject to appropriate & just qualification, there is NO limit to remedies that may be ordered under Ss. 24(1). They include “defensive” remedies, where the court nullifies or stops some law or act, eg, by dismissing a charge, staying a proceeding, quashing a search warrant or a committal or a conviction, enjoining an act or declaring a law to be invalid. The exclusion of evidence obtained in breach of the Charter also falls into the defensive category, BUT the exclusion of evidence is subject to a special set of rules under Ss. 24(2). The remedies also include “affirmative” remedies, such as: o ordering a province to provide state-funded counsel to an indigent litigant: New Brunswick v G(J) [1999]; o ordering the return of goods improperly seized: RE Chapman (1984) (CA); or o a mandatory injunction requiring positive action.

It has been suggested that the court’s remedial discretion should be governed by 4 factors: 1. 2. 3. 4. the redress of the wrong suffered by the applicant; the encouragement of future compliance with the Constitution; the avoidance of unnecessary interference with the exercise of governmental power; and the ability of the court to administer the remedy awarded.

243

(g.1) Declaration Re Gamble [1988]; Vancouver v Ward [2010]  A simple declaration that the gov’t is in default of its Charter duties would almost invariably be obeyed and would therefore usually be an effective remedy  Especially appropriate if court not sure what would be the appropriate remedy by gov’t Canada v Khadr [2010]      A Canadian citizen, who was being held for trial by the US at Guantanamo Bay, applied to the Federal Court for an order that Canada request the US to return him to Canada. The accused was alleged to have killed an American soldier in Afghanistan and the evidence against him included the record of interviews by Canadian officials. SC held that the Canadian officials had acted in breach of the Charter and that the breach contributed to the accused’s ongoing detention by the US. BUT the SC refused to order Canada to request the return of the accused to Canada. SC recognized that making of representations to foreign gov’ts was a complex matter upon which the courts should defer to executive branch. SC granted only a declaration that the accused’s Charter rights had been infringed, thereby leaving to the gov’t “a measure of discretion in deciding how best to respond”

(g.2) Damages *** Vancouver v Ward [2010] - Unanimous      The P, a lawyer, was mistakenly identified by the police as a person who was planning to throw a pie at the PM, who was attending a public ceremony in Vancouver. City police confronted P and handcuffed him and when he protested and created as scene, they arrested him for a breach of the peace and took him to a police lock-up, where provincial corrections officers took charge of him. Strip-searched & held him in a cell for more than four hours before releasing him without laying any charges. During this time, the police also seized his car, but realized that they did not have enough evidence to obtain a warrant to search it and returned it to him when he was released. The P sued and at trial was awarded damages under Ss. 24(1) of $5,000 from the province for the strip search in breach of Ss. 8 (unreasonable search or seizure) and of $100 from the city for the seizure of the car (another breach of Ss. 8). (He was also awarded damages of $5,000 from the city for the tort of wrongful imprisonment). The awards of Charter damages (not the tort damages) were appealed on up to the SC, which agreed that the P’s rights under Ss. 8 had been infringed and upheld the award of damages for the strip search, BUT held that a declaration was a sufficient remedy for the seizure of the car. McLachlin CJ for the Court held that damages was an appropriate and just remedy for a breach of the Charter when they served a useful function, ie, to: 1. 2. 3.       compensate the P for his loss; vindicate Charter rights; or deter future Charter breaches.

 

Because vindication and deterrence pursued societal goals, Charter damages under Ss. 24(1) would NOT necessarily be the same as CL damages (which are purely compensatory). Even when no personal loss was suffered, a damages award might be appropriate and just under Ss. 24(1) SC upheld TJ’s award of $5,000 damages for the strip search on ground that his humiliating experience should receive some compensation and that the functions of vindication and deterrence also justified some damages. However, the SC showed NO sympathy for merely symbolic awards of damages and reversed TJ’s award of $100 for the seizure of P’s car: this caused the P NO harm, the car was NOT searched and, although the seizure was a breach of Ss. 8, it was NOT a serious one. For that breach, the remedy of a declaration, which was sufficient to fulfil the functions of vindication and deterrence, was all that should be granted. However, even when damages are “functionally required” to fulfil one or more of the objects of compensation, vindication or deterrence, there could be countervailing considerations that would render an award of damages inappropriate and unjust.

244

    

Eg, the availability of alternative remedies. “The Charter entered an existent remedial area which already housed tools to correct violative state conduct”. Here, this was illustrated with a simple (yet sufficient) declaration for the seizure of the car. Further, IF private actions in tort would adequately address the Charter breach, THEN ‘Charter damages would be duplicative and should not be awarded. The CJ said that a tort claim could be joined with a Charter claim and it would be “useful” for the court to consider the tort claim FIRST in case it rendered recourse to Ss. 24(1) unnecessary; BUT it was NOT “essential” to exhaust the tort remedies first. Here, the P’s tort action for wrongful imprisonment was successful and yielded damages of $5,000 for his detention in the police lock-up; on appeal, he did NOT pursue any additional Charter damages for the detention. No tort damages were awarded for the strip search or the seizure for the car and so no issue of duplicative remedies arose for those causes of action. First, it will be for the plaintiff to establish that his/her Charter rights have been infringed. Second, and more substantially, the plaintiff MUST demonstrate that damages will fulfill one or more of the objects of Ss. 24(1) damages: o (1) compensating the claimant for loss and suffering; o (2) vindicating the right by emphasizing its importance and the gravity of the breach; and o (3) deterring state agents from committing future breaches. The claimant need NOT show that she has exhausted ALL other resources; IF the plaintiff can show a breach and a prima facie case that damages are “appropriate and just”, the burden will then shift to the state to establish countervailing factors that demonstrate that damages are inappropriate or unjust.

Damages are awarded based on a 2 stage, multi-part test:  



Mackin v NB [2002]  Another concern is the “concern for effective governance”. No damages are available “for the harm suffered as the result of the mere enactment or application of a law that is subsequently held to be unconstitutional” Guimond v Que [1996]  Ss. 24(1) does NOT authorize damages for harm caused by gov’t officials who act in good faith and w/o negligence under the “claim of right” of statute subsequently held to be unconstitutional for Charter breach  According to McLachlin CJ, these doctrines are exemplary of the principle of good governance, because damages awards would “chill the exercise of policy-making discretion” and deter public officials from carrying out their duties under apparently valid statutes  McLachlin CJ described damages under Ss. 24(1) as “a unique public law remedy”; it was NOT “a private law action in the nature of a tort claim”. For one thing, the Charter action “lies against the state and NOT against individual actors”.  Since individual actors are not bound by the Charter, they are not liable for damages under Ss. 24(1) and the crown is not liable vicariously for their Charter breaches.  The Crown is liable directly (not vicariously) for breaches of the Charter. Any personal liability of individual actors such as police or corrections officers would have to be found in the private law of tort and, if so found, the Crown would be vicariously liable for the tort, provided it was committed by an official in the course of employment. (g.3) Costs  Default in the Crown’s Charter obligation to make pre-trial disclosure to a criminal D has often been sanctioned by an award of costs against the Crown. A stay of proceedings would normally be too severe a sanction and a mere adjournment not severe enough. R v 974649 [2001]  The SC has warned that this remedy was appropriate and just only in exceptional cases: “costs awards will not flow from every failure to disclose in a timely fashion”, but are restricted to “circumstances of a marked and unacceptable departure from the reasonable standards expected of the prosecution” (g.4) Exclusion of evidence  Evidence that has been obtained in compliance with the Charter is NOT covered by Ss. 24(2) (exclusion of evidence) and yet in SOME situations the exclusion of the evidence WILL be an appropriate and just remedy under Ss. 24(1).

245

R v White [1999]   An accident report, given to the police by the accused under the (valid) compulsion of a provincial law that REQ reporting of all serious traffic accidents, was excluded from accused’s trial for leaving the scene of the accident. The exclusion was ordered under Ss. 24(1) on the ground that the admission of the accused’s report would offend one of the principles of fundamental justice under Ss. 7, namely, the principle against self-incrimination.

R v Bjelland [2009] o o o o o o o In criminal trial for drug offences, Crown, after making timely disclosure to the defence of what was thought to be most of the evidence, discovered and disclosed further evidence shortly before the date set for trial. The TJ excluded the late-breaking evidence for breach of the Charter obligation of timely disclosure (under Ss 7: R v Stinchcombe [1991]). The trial proceeded and the accused was acquitted. The SC ordered that the evidence be admitted and a new trial held. Rothstein J, for the maj, held that the TJ erred in excluding the evidence when the less drastic remedy of an adjournment was available as a remedy under Ss. 24(1). Because the exclusion of relevant evidence impairs the truth-seeking function of a trial, the NORMAL remedy for a Crown default in timely disclosure should be an adjournment of the trial to give the defence time to consider the new evidence. The exclusion of the new evidence should be ordered ONLY in exceptional cases affecting the fairness of the trial or the integrity of the justice system. Eg of exceptional cases would be where the postponement of the trial would lead to a breach of the accused’s Charter right to be tried within a reasonable time or where the accused was in pre- trial custody and the postponement of the trial would significantly prolong the custody. Another possible (but less clear) example would be where the Crown had abused the process by deliberately withholding the evidence from the accused. None of these exceptional circumstances were present here, so the prejudice to the accused would have been cured by the less drastic remedy of postponing the trial.

o o

(g.5) Remedies outside Ss 24(1) R v Nasogaluak [2010] o The accused had pleaded guilty and been convicted of impaired driving and fleeing from the police, offences that would normally attract a sentence of imprisonment. Instead, the TJ granted him a 12-month conditional discharge coupled with a 12-month driving prohibition. The police had used excessive force in making the arrest and in preventing flight and they had inflicted injuries on the accused.
