The TJ held that the police actions were a breach of the accused’s Ss. 7 right to security of the person and a reduced sentence was an appropriate and just remedy for the breach under Ss. 24(1) SC agreed that there had been a breach of Ss. 7 and that it justified a reduction of the accused’s sentence. However, SC held that the TJ had been wrong to rely on Ss. 24(1) as the justification for the reduced sentence. Sentence reduction was NOT an appropriate remedy under Ss. 24(1), except in the exceptional case where it was the “sole effective remedy” for a Charter breach However, under normal sentencing principles, a Charter breach that related to the circumstances of the offence or the offender could properly be taken into account in sentencing. Even state misconduct that did not amount to a Charter breach could be taken into account in sentencing. Here, the police breach of the accused’s Charter rights was properly taken into account in fixing the accused’s sentence without the need to invoke Ss. 24(1). The SC upheld the reduced sentence.

o

o o o

o

(h) Supervision of court orders Doucet-Boudreau v Nova Scotia [2003] o o o A superior-court judge in NS, acting on the application of French-speaking parents, found that in 5 districts of the province, the numbers of children of French-speaking parents warranted French- language schools. Under the authority of Ss. 24(1), judge ordered that 5 schools be built and that programs of instruction be developed for each school. Stipulated dates by which each school should be built and its curriculum developed. He also declared that “the Court shall retain jurisdiction to hear reports from [the government] respecting [the government’s] compliance with the order”. Pursuant to this, the judge held a series of reporting hearings throughout the period of construction of the schools, at which the government was required to file affidavits as to the progress of its work, the parents were permitted to file affidavits of rebuttal and all deponents were subject to cross-examination.

246

o o o o o o o o o o

FIRST time a Canadian court had made provision for judicial supervision of compliance with a Ss. 24(1) order. AG of NS appealed the provision for judicial supervision SC majority (5:4) upheld the provision for judicial supervision. Iacobucci and Arbour JJ, for the maj, pointed out that the Charter was supposed to receive a large and liberal construction and this applied to the remedial power of Ss. 24(1) no less than to the substantive rights. Re “appropriate and just in the circumstances”, they said it was “difficult to imagine language which could give the court a wider and less fettered discretion” Remedies under Ss. 24(1) could include “novel and creative features when compared with traditional and historical remedial practice” The majority repeated the TJ’s concerns about the danger of progressive assimilation of the French-speaking majority in the province and past delays in developing French-language schools. TJ concerned that, w/o the reporting requirement, the order to build the schools would be “ineffective” The supervisory order was appropriate and just in the circumstances and upheld as a valid exercise of Ss. 24(1). The minority thought the judge was functus officio after the order to build the school was made.

Hogg argues minority view right that such remedy should be last resort. ONLY where governmental non-compliance with judicial orders does such last resort course of action have merit Abdelrazik v Canada (2009) [2010] (Federal Court) (Zinn J) o o o o o A Canadian citizen, who had made a visit to Sudan (the land of his birth), was arrested without charge, imprisoned for several years and tortured by the Sudanese authorities. When he was released, his Canadian passport had expired and efforts to obtain a new one, even just a temporary one to get him back home to Montreal to his family, all proved unavailing. The applicant brought an application for a declaration that his right to enter Canada under Ss. 6(1) of the Charter had been violated and for an appropriate and just remedy for the violation under Ss. 24(1) The Fed Ct found that the Canadian gov’t had been complicit in the applicant’s arrest and imprisonment (but not in the torture) by Sudan. The FC also found that, after the applicant’s release from prison in Sudan, when he was staying in the Canadian embassy, the Canadian gov’t in Ottawa, while giving him assurances of aid and comfort, had actually been doing its best to block his return to Canada. SC held that applicant had a constitutional right to return to Cdn under Ss. 6(1) & that the right had been violated. The FC ordered that an emergency passport be issued to enable the applicant to return to Canada. But, based on the past history of governmental bad faith in dealing with the applicant, the FC also ordered that the applicant be brought before the FC after his return for the purpose of satisfying the FC that he had in fact returned to Canada. (There was also another reason for the order: the applicant was the subject of a UN travel ban to which an exception was the fulfillment of a “judicial process” And, in case that did not happen in a timely fashion, the Court reserved the right “to issue further orders as may be required to safely return Mr Abdelrazik to Canada”.

o o

o o

(i) Appeals Mills v The Queen [1986] o Section 24(1) does NOT authorize an appeal from the decision of a court of competent jurisdiction Where there is NO existing right of appeal, as will often be the case in applications in criminal proceedings before the trial is over, there will be NO appeal from the Charter ruling by the court of competent jurisdiction. Knox Contracting v Canada [1990] o o Maj SC held that there was NO appeal from refusal by a judge to quash search warrant issued under Income Tax Act. SC held that, because neither the Income Tax Act nor the Criminal Code provided for any right of appeal, there was no right of appeal, despite the fact that the applicant claimed that the warrant had been issued in breach of the Charter.

Kourtessis v Minister of National Revenue [1993] o o The applicant applied for a declaration that a statutory provision in the Income Tax Act authorizing a warrant and the warrant itself were unconstitutional for breach of the Charter. SC unanimous that an appeal WAS available in these proceedings.

247

o o o

An application (or action) for a declaration of invalidity was a civil proceeding. The declaration “is NOT transformed from a civil remedy to a criminal remedy merely because the declaration relates to a criminal statutory provision”. It followed that the application for a declaration in this case was governed by the provincial rules of civil procedure, which made provision for an appeal. In this way, the rules of criminal procedure were bypassed, although the Court emphasized that there would be a discretion to refuse to issue the declaration if another procedure were available to resolve the constitutional issue.

(j) Limitation of actions R v Thomas [1990] o Where proceedings are brought, whether under Ss. 24(1) or under the general law, for a “personal remedy”, such as the recovery of unconstitutional taxes or damages for breach of the Charter, statutes of limitation of general application will apply to the proceedings. Indeed, the SC has required persons convicted of unconstitutional crimes to serve out their time in prison if they were outside the time limits for appeals from conviction

o

Kingstreet Investments v New Brunswick [2007] o o o o The Ps, taxpayers, brought an action against the Crown in right of NB to recover taxes that had been imposed by the provincial Leg without constitutional authority. Section 24(1) was NOT applicable because there had been no breach of the Charter. The Ps simply relied on the law of restitution to recover the taxes that had unjustly enriched the Crown. SC held that the Ps were entitled to recover the taxes under the law of restitution, BUT the SC held that the recovery was subject to the province’s limitation statute, which included a residuary limitation period (for all actions not otherwise provided for) of 6 years after the cause of action arose. That period applied to the P’s action and their recovery was accordingly limited to those taxes that had been paid during the 6-year period preceding the commencement of the action. Bastarache J, for the SC, distinguished the Court’s earlier decision in Amax Potash v Govt of Saskatchewan [1977], where the SC had struck down a provincial law that purported to bar the recovery of unconstitutional taxes. The bar had been enacted for the sole purpose of denying relief for things done under an unconstitutional law. Bastarache J drew a distinction between a law that was enacted for the purpose of barring the recovery of an unconstitutional tax from the Crown (Amax Potash) and a law of general application that imposed a limitation period on a class of generally-described causes of action. There was NO REASON why a general limitation statute should not be effective to bar stale claims for the recovery of unconstitutional taxes

o o

o

o

Ravndahl v Saskatchewan [2009] o o o o o The widow of a person who had been killed in the course of his employment received a workers compensation pension from 1975, the date of her husband’s death, until 1984, when she remarried. On her remarriage, the workers compensation legislation of that time terminated her pension. SC held that the termination of her pension was a violation of Ss. 15 of the Charter and that she had a cause of action against the Crown in right of Saskatchewan under Ss. 24(1) for damages for breach of the Charter. However, that cause of action arose on April 17, 1985, when Ss. 15 came into force. Following Kingstreet, the Court held that her proceedings, which had been commenced in 2000, were defeated by the province’s limitation statute, which imposed a residuary limitation period (for all actions not otherwise provided for) of six years after the cause of action arose. The limitation period did NOT bar her actions for declarations that the applicable legislation was unconstitutional. The SC did not address the problem that recognising that she had a cause of action involved giving retroactive effect to the Charter, since the remarriage and consequent termination of the pension had taken place before the coming into force of Ss 15

o o

Prete v Ontario (1993) (CA) o o o The P brought an action for damages under Ss. 24(1) alleging various Charter breaches by the law officers of the Crown, who had prosecuted him unsuccessfully. The P brought his action outside the 6-month limitation period that was then prescribed for proceedings against the Crown and public officials by Ontario’s Public Authorities Protection Act. The Act had NOT been passed for the specific purpose of barring constitutional claims and it was not limited to constitutional claims.

248

o o o o o o

However, the Act was hardly a law of general application, since it applied ONLY to proceedings against public authorities (including the Crown) and imposed a very short limitation period on those proceedings. The Ont CA held that the limitation period did NOT bar the action. Carthy JA, for the Court on this issue, explained that governments should not, even by statute, be permitted “to decide when they would like to be free of [constitutional] controls” The ONLY limitation Carthy JA suggested was the doctrine of laches. Here, the action had been commenced within 18 months, which he evidently did not consider to be the kind of unreasonable delay that would amount to laches. The result was that the P was allowed to proceed with his action, although an action framed in tort for malicious prosecution (which the P’s constitutional claim closely resembled) would have been statute-barred. Prete decided before Kingstreet and Ravndahl and not referred to by the SC in either of those cases.

Hogg argues that Prete was rightly decide, because the Crown should NOT be allowed to immunize itself from constitutional controls by enacting special, short limitation periods for proceedings against itself.

40.3 Administrative Tribunals
(a) With power to decide questions of law Douglas/Kwantlen Faculty Association v Douglas College [1990] o SC held that an arbitration board, which had been appointed by the parties under a collective agreement, BUT which was empowered by statute to decide questions of law, HAD the power to determine the constitutionality of a mandatory retirement provision in the collective agreement. o La Forest J, for the SC, said: “A tribunal MUST respect the Constitution so that if it finds invalid a law that is called upon to apply, it is BOUND to treat it as having no force or effect”. o This conclusion was entailed by the supremacy clause in Ss. 52(1) of the CA, 1982. o In effect, the tribunal was obliged to apply ALL of the relevant law, which included the Constitution as well as the relevant portions of contracts and statutes. o Thus, in Douglas College, the professors who had been mandatorily retired were entitled to be reinstated only if the mandatory retirement provision was invalid.

Cuddy Chicks v Ontario [1991] o SC held that a labour relations board, which had been created and empowered by statute to decide questions of law, had the power to determine the constitutionality of a provision in the empowering statute that denied collective bargaining rights to agricultural workers. o The union that had organized the agricultural workers WAS entitled to certification only if the agricultural workers exclusion was invalid. ONLY “superior courts” have the power to issue binding declarations of invalidity that will invalidate a law with general effect

o

In both cases o o o It was necessary for the tribunal to resolve the constitutional issue in order to exercise its statutory jurisdiction. SC expressly refused to decide whether an administrative trial could be a court of competent jurisdiction under Ss. 24(1) Section 24(1) was irrelevant: the applicants were NOT seeking any remedy for breach of the Charter. The application was seeking a remedy that the tribunal was empowered to grant in the normal course of its jurisdiction, namely, the reinstatement of wrongfully dismissed employees in Douglas College and the certification of a union in Cuddy Chicks. The Charter issue arose in the course of determining whether the remedy should be granted, because the Charter, if applicable, directed the tribunal to disregard a law that would otherwise bar the tribunal from granting the remedy sought.

o

o o o

Once Ss. 52(1) had invalidated the apparently applicable law, the subject matter and the remedy would give to the applicants all that they asked for. La Forest J acknowledged the tribunal’s expert knowledge of the regulated field, but added that constitutional determinations by administrative tribunals should receive “no curial deference” In other words, the standard of judicial review is correctness. Superior court can decide for itself even where lower court/tribunal within correct scope – NOT deference

249

Where an administrative tribunal decides a constitutional question, its decision will be subject to judicial review by a superior court. Indeed, Hogg argues, an attempt by Parl’t or a Leg to enact a privative clause to bar judicial review would be unconstitutional.

o o

The SC has insisted, however, that an administrative tribunal has no power to make a declaration of invalidity: Nova Scotia v Martin [2003] The SC has held that the absence of an express power to decide questions of law precluded an administrative tribunal from deciding Charter issues: Tétreault-Gadoury v Can [1991] 2 SCR 222; Cooper v Can [1996] 3 SCR 854 The SC has now repudiated the two rulings: Nova Scotia v Martin (2003).

o

Nova Scotia v Martin [2003] o o o o o o o o The Workers’ Compensation Appeals Tribunal of NS faced a claim that the benefits provided by NS’s workers’ compensation plan for chronic pain were unconstitutional for violation of Ss. 15. SC held that the Tribunal HAD the power to rule on the Charter issue. The Tribunal had express power to determine questions of law, BUT Court took opportunity to “reappraise & restate” law SC held that a tribunal with power to determine q’s of law, whether the power was express or implied, was presumed to have the power to determine the constitutional validity of any potentially applicable law. That presumption could be rebutted ONLY by showing that the leg’n empowering the tribunal “clearly intended to exclude Charter issues from the tribunal’s authority over questions of law”. That clear intention would normally be evidenced by legislative provision for an alternative route for the resolution of Charter issues coming before the tribunal. Here, there was nothing in the Tribunal’s empowering leg’n that indicated an intention to exclude Charter issues from the Tribunal’s authority. It followed that the Tribunal could decide the Charter issue. The SC, applying its review standard of correctness, went on to hold that the chronic pain provisions were contrary to the Charter.

Paul v BC [2003] o Was a law unconstitutional for breach of the aboriginal rights guaranteed by Ss. 35 of the AC, 1982? This q’s came before the Forest Appeals Commission of BC, which had to determine whether Mr Paul, a registered Indian, had violated the statutory Forest Practices Code of the province. o Mr Paul had cut down 3 trees on Crown land, intending to use the timber to build a deck on his home. The Code prohibited the cutting of Crown timber, but Mr Paul asserted that he had an aboriginal right to harvest the trees. o The Commission decided that it had the power to deal with this defence, but Mr Paul immediately sought judicial review to stop the Commission from determining his aboriginal rights. o The SC held that the power of an administrative tribunal to determine whether a law was overridden by an aboriginal right was governed by the same rules as Martin stipulated were to be applied to Charter issues. o Here, the commission had the power, under its empowering statute, to decide questions of law. That power was presumed to include the power to determine whether a potentially applicable law was unconstitutional in its application to an Indian by reason of Ss. 25 of the CA, 1982. o There was nothing in the empowering statute to indicate an intention to withdraw aboriginal rights issues from the jurisdiction of the Commission. o Therefore, the Commission had the power to hear and determine Mr Paul’s defence of aboriginal right and the Commission should resume its proceeding in order to receive his evidence and determine the issue. o Applicant argued that the provincial Leg could not enact a law that had the effect of empowering a tribunal to determine questions relating to aboriginal rights. That would encroach on the federal power over “Indians, and lands reserved for the Indians” in Ss. 91(24) of the CA, 1867 – encroaching on the “core of ‘Indianness’”. o The SC rejected this argument on the basis that adjudication was distinct from legislation. o The power conferred by the BC Leg on its Forest Appeals Commission was SOLELY an adjudicative one. The Commission was not granted the power to alter or extinguish aboriginal rights; it could ONLY determine whether they existed. o The Commission’s determinations would be binding on the parties, BUT they would not constitute precedents that were binding on other tribunals or courts and they would be subject to judicial review on a standard of correctness o Bastarache J drew analogy of provincial court, which has jurisdiction to apply entire body of law, including federal law & constitutional law, to resolve disputes properly before it; a provincial administrative tribunal with power to adjudicate questions of law must also take account of all applicable legal rules, whether provincial, federal or constitutional.

250

Tranchemontagne v Ontario [2006] o The claimant argued a provision of the empowering statute of the Ontario’s Social Benefits Tribunal, which denied a disability benefit (but not general social assistance) to a person whose incapacity resulted from addiction to alcohol or drugs, was inoperative for conflict with the Ontario Human Rights Code on the basis that the provision was discriminatory. The Code is expressly given primacy over other Ontario statutes and these primacy clauses have been held to be effective, which gives a “quasi-constitutional” status to human rights codes. The Tribunal, which had power to determine income-support decisions on appeal from a statutory official, held that it had no power to entertain this argument and denied the disability benefit on the ground of the claimant’s addiction. The tribunal’s empowering statute authorized it to decide questions of law, BUT expressly denied the power to determine the “constitutional validity” of a statute or regulation. Did this restriction on its powers deny to the Tribunal the power to determine whether the addiction provision was contrary to the Code? Bastarache J, for maj, said ‘no’. While Tribunal was precluded from “invalidating” a statutory provision on constitutional grounds, it was NOT precluded from refusing to apply a statutory provision on the ground of conflict with the Code. Aside from the constitutional issues that were expressly withdraw from its powers, the Tribunal was empowered to apply “the whole law” and that included the Code and its primacy clause.

o o o o o o

(b) Without power to decide questions of law o o Administrative tribunals that lack the power to decide questions of law also lack the power to refuse to apply laws on the ground of unconstitutionality. MOST administrative tribunals w/ adjudicative functions will possess the power to decide questions of law.

(c) Preliminary inquiry judge R v Seaboyer [1991] o SC held that a judge presiding at a preliminary inquiry of an indictable offence had no jurisdiction under Ss. 52(1) of the CA, 1982 to determine the constitutionality of a rape-shield law which purported to limit the accused’s right to crossexamine the victim of a sexual assault. SC acknowledged that the preliminary inquiry judge would in the course of conducting the inquiry have the power (and the duty) to rule on the admissibility of the evidence presented at the inquiry. BUT this power did NOT extend to determining the constitutionality of a statute that purported to prohibit the admission of evidence. In order to determine whether there was sufficient evidence to make the accused stand trial, the prelim inquiry judge was obliged “to accept the rules of evidence as they stand”. Any Charter challenge to the rules of evidence had to await the trial, where the TJ would be permitted to look at the CC as part of the body of law to be applied.

o o o o

Hogg points out that the dec’n in Seaboyer predates the decisions in Martin and Paul, which hold that the power to decide questions of law raises the presumption that the decision-maker also has the power to determine the constitutionality of any potentially applicable. Therefore, Seaboyer is inconsistent with those decisions. o The Court did not make any mention of Seaboyer in the later decisions, BUT Hogg argues that it is safe to regard Seaboyer as impliedly overruled.

40.4 Scrutiny by Minister of Justice
o o o Charter makes NO provision for the pre-enactment scrutiny of proposed statutes and regulations. AS OPPOSED TO THE BILL OF RIGHTS BUT 1985 Department of Justice Act was amended to include MoJ’s scrutiny to encompass Charter as well as Bill

40.5 Legislative Enforcement
o o o Section 31 of the Charter declares that “NOTHING in this Charter extends the legislative powers of any body or authority”. It is CLEAR then that no remedial or enforcement power in the federal Parl’t is to be inferred from the provisions of the Charter. Enforcement of the Charter is the function of the courts, by virtue of Ss. 52(1) or Ss. 24
